This sounds like a conspiracy, but virtually all of the popes since the 19th century have publicly and explicitly been attacking modernism and freemasonry. Many papal encyclicals have been written in condemnation of modernism, freemasonry and also the separation of church and state. 

However, by the time Pope Pius XII died in the 1950s, the whole Church had slowly been filled with modernist thinking priests and theologians who wanted to be joined with the Protestants and other religions. At the Second Vatican Council in the 1960s the traditional Mass was thrown away, new sacraments and new ordination rites for the priests were created, and a new Church structure began. 

Many traditional Catholics pointed to Catholic doctrine that heretics cannot be popes, and have since denounced all elected popes who implement the new Second Vatican teachings and sacraments. These traditional Catholics (sedevecantists) are a small minority.

John Paul 2 is the prime example of this, as is Francis bringing an aztec mother goddess into St Peter’s Basilica. 

The new pope is not a traditional Catholic. He is part of the same modern ecumenical brand of New Catholicism that the popes since the 1960s have all belonged to. 

That being said, there is a small but vocal group of more traditional minded Catholics within the modern Church who want a return to the true traditional Catholic faith. Professor Jiang: Many thanks for your exceptional efforts. I hope you’ll feel encouraged to keep up your singluar efforts. To the point: are eschatologies purely religious? I think that cannot be because ‘religion’ is itself not what it seems. Our fundamental concept of modernity has been well characterised as ‘secularisation’ and that means ‘religion by other means’ so to say. We should take a more critical look at sociological theories. The three major theories are Liberalism, Socialism, and Nationalism. Liberalism is the secular destiny of Protestant individualism, Socialism is Christian brotherliness in atheistic terms mainly, and Nationalism at it’s core signifies numerous attempts to revive some sort of ‘natural religion’. But be that as it may, all three are eschatological. They each answer your three questions. An ideology is something to live for .. and die for to. Much more could be said about this but I’ll leave it at that for the present. Again, thanks for everything.

Thanks for the lecture, Professor Jiang — I’m an educator and definitely still learning when it comes to this space, but I found your framework really compelling.

That said, I’ve been reading Peter Zeihan lately, and I’m trying to square some of your predictions with his take. Zeihan basically argues that Russia’s cooked long-term — aging population, crumbling infrastructure, overextended geography — while Turkey is actually set up well to rise as a regional power due to demographics and control of key maritime routes.

Your view seems to flip that. I’m curious how your eschatological lens accounts for those structural realities — or if you think Zeihan’s missing something bigger, maybe ideological or even spiritual, that reshapes the map in ways his models don’t catch?

### **@KaFeCocoa**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YQ-xg1nIbMs&lc=Ugzbgeah7oz8n5tYvEp4AaABAg)

CGTN Interviews Alexander Dugin & His Views on China-Russia Relations​​
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I0fM2CVGk10

INTERVIEW SUMMARY: He's so impressed by what China has done for its people, that he's ready to reset his beliefs about the China/Russia partnership.
Alexander Dugin is a ​​Russian political philosopher​​ who used to believe:

✔ ​​A strong Russia-led alliance against Western dominance​​
✔ ​​A "multipolar world" where the U.S. and Europe lose global control​​

✔ ​​Close ties between Russia, China, and other non-Western powers​​



He is often called ​​"Putin’s Brain"​​ because his ideas have influenced Russian foreign policy, though his exact role in the Kremlin is debated.



​​What Did Dugin Say About China in His CGTN Interview?​​

In his ​​2024 interview with CGTN​​, Dugin discussed ​​BRICS (Brazil, Russia, India, China, South Africa)​​ and how it represents a ​​new world order​​ where the West is no longer dominant.



​​Key Points from the Interview:​​

​​BRICS = A "Multipolar World"​​

Dugin says BRICS is ​​not just an economic group​​ but a ​​new global alliance​​ of ​​civilization-states​​ (China, Russia, India, Brazil, etc.).



He argues that ​​the West (U.S. & EU) should be excluded​​ from global decision-making because of its history of "crimes against humanity."



​​Russia’s Role in BRICS​​

Russia is ​​chairing BRICS in 2024​​, and Dugin believes Russia should ​​focus on promoting "multipolar philosophy"​​ rather than just economic deals.



He wants BRICS to ​​replace Western-led institutions like the UN​​ and create a ​​new world order without U.S. dominance​​.



​​China & Russia’s Growing Alliance​​

Dugin sees ​​China as a key partner​​ in this new world order.



He praises ​​Xi Jinping’s "Global Civilization Initiative"​​ (a Chinese plan for cultural cooperation outside Western influence).



He suggests that ​​Russia and China must work together​​ to ​​defeat Western globalism​​.



​​Did Dugin Say He Had to "Rethink" China-Russia Differences?​​

​​No, he did not say that directly in this interview.​​ However, his tone shows ​​strong support for China-Russia rapprochement (closer ties)​​.



In the past, Dugin was ​​more skeptical of China​​, fearing it might overshadow Russia.



​​But now​​, he seems to ​​fully embrace China as a partner​​ in the fight against the West.



​​Why the Change?​​

​​The Ukraine War Made Russia More Dependent on China​​



After Western sanctions, Russia ​​needs China for trade, technology, and political support​​.



Dugin now sees China as ​​an essential ally​​, not a rival.



​​BRICS Expansion Shows a United Front Against the West​​



With new members like ​​Iran, Saudi Arabia, and Egypt​​, BRICS is becoming a ​​powerful anti-Western bloc​​.



Dugin believes this is ​​Russia and China’s best chance to weaken U.S. power​​.



​​Xi Jinping’s Vision Aligns with Dugin’s Eurasian Dream​​



Both want a ​​world where the U.S. is no longer the top superpower​​.



Dugin now sees China’s rise as ​​helpful, not threatening​​, to Russia’s goals.



​​Dugin’s Real Views on China-Russia Relations​​

✔ ​​Before:​​ Dugin was ​​cautious​​—he worried China might dominate Eurasia instead of Russia.



✔ ​​Now:​​ He ​​fully supports​​ the China-Russia alliance, seeing it as ​​the only way to defeat the West​​.



​​His New Stance:​​

​​China and Russia must unite​​ to ​​build a multipolar world​​.



​​The West must be pushed out​​ of global leadership.

Hi, can you please shortly answer to my questions down below here based on your all lecture about current geopolitical situation in this video?

A.  “The Catholic Church was founded by a conspiracy”
	1.	Primary-source challenge:
“Which first-century text outside the New Testament names the specific individuals who allegedly conspired to invent the Church, and where can we read the full passage?”
— Anything later than Ignatius of Antioch (c. 110 AD) or the Didache pushes the goalposts.  ￼ ￼
	2.	Chain-of-custody test:
“Show the unbroken documentary chain—from 1st-century origin to 4th-century imperial adoption—that records this plot without relying on later hearsay.”
	3.	Co-conspirator logistics:
“How did dozens of Jewish sectarians coordinate a secret global project across the Roman Empire when most could neither travel freely nor control imperial communications?”
	4.	Motive interrogation:
“What political or financial payoff in 33–70 AD required inventing a new religion rather than capturing influence inside Judaism?”
	5.	Archaeology cross-check:
“Which archaeological layers (inscriptions, house-churches, burial practices) contradict the standard, organic-growth timeline endorsed by secular scholars?”
	6.	Occam’s Razor:
“Explain why a single-stroke conspiracy is a simpler explanation than spontaneous, decentralized Christian communities that we can track in the record.”

⸻

B.  “There is a strong CIA–Vatican collaboration today”
	7.	Document dump request:
“Cite a declassified memorandum of understanding (MOU) signed after 2001 that establishes a standing CIA–Holy See liaison office.  Where is it archived?”
	8.	Budgetary paper trail:
“Point to a U.S. Congressional budget line—or an ASIF† annual report—showing ongoing joint funding for intelligence programs.” (ASIF is the Vatican’s financial-intelligence unit.)
	9.	Operational specifics:
“Name one covert action since 2010 openly attributed to both the CIA and a Vatican organ, and provide the supporting declassified cables.”
	10.	Personnel overlap:
“List current Vatican officials holding U.S. security clearances or CIA embeds who travel on Vatican passports.  What’s your source?”
	11.	Legal-status hurdle:
“How does the collaboration square with the U.S. Foreign Missions Act, which channels nearly all official Vatican contact through the State Department, not the CIA?”
	12.	Comparative scale question:
“Is the Holy See’s liaison any deeper than CIA ties with, say, Sweden or Malaysia?  Show metrics (frequency of joint task-forces, intel exchanges, etc.).”
	13.	Counter-example probe:
“Why did the Vatican oppose core U.S. positions on the 2003 Iraq War and the 2020s Gaza policy if the two are supposedly ‘strongly’ aligned?”

⸻

C.  “The Catholic Church is the most influential institution of the past 2,000 years”
	14.	Influence-index demand:
“Define your yard-sticks—population governed, territory controlled, laws shaped—and show comparative data versus the Chinese imperial bureaucracy, the British Empire, or Islam’s Caliphates.”
	15.	Longevity vs. dominance:
“Does uninterrupted existence automatically equal ‘most influential,’ or do shorter-lived entities (e.g., British Empire, Soviet Union) that radically redrew maps count for more in your model?”
	16.	Causal-mechanism clarification:
“Identify three measurable levers (education systems, financial assets, diplomatic agreements) through which the Church currently exerts influence that exceeds that of the United Nations or Big Tech.”
	17.	Negative-case check:
“If the Church’s influence is paramount, how do we explain its failure to stop secular legislation such as same-sex marriage in dozens of historically Catholic states?”
	18.	Historiography audit:
“Which peer-reviewed global-history meta-studies rank the Church #1 by influence—and on what criteria?  Provide citations.”

Thanks.

Lukas

### **@Ryd1446**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YQ-xg1nIbMs&lc=UgyIBbIE9n7EaOhp-_94AaABAg)

I find these frameworks quite beneficial. Although, as someone who has studied Islamic eschatology, I have to be honest that the professor is making several errors in eschatological events, especially with the reconciliation part at the end with Putin/Orthodox empire. Oftentimes, Islamic eschatology just gets thrown under the bus as “basically the same as Judeo-Christian” eschatology. Although many names are similar, the understanding, theology, and different factions of who is who is completely different. I understand he’s trying to converge them into one theory of what will really happen in his own prediction. Essentially, these eschatologies are not in themselves real to the professor, but self-fulfilling prophecies which causes different parties to act within the interest of causing all the events that would lead to their eschatology to come about. However, I would disagree with approach because in trying to fit each eschatology with modern government powers and leaders, it inevitably leads to stretching prophecies way beyond their apparent meaning, which is why we see the professor resort to making prophecies very metaphorical and more figurative than is plausible. As a concrete example, he mentioned the Muslim world would accept Putin as the leader/force of peace; however, Islamic eschatology never mentions that this will happen; rather, it says the opposite (i.e. that the Muslims would unite under the Mahdi and fight a great war against all the non-believing nations who are united against them, and then they will have 7 years of prosperity after winning the war, then the anti-Christ comes, then Jesus’ second coming would be to the Muslims side and Jesus will kill the anti-Christ, then Gog and Magog (a literal physical group of people) will appear and kill everyone except for a group of Muslims alongside Jesus hiding in a mountain, then a disease from God will kill Gog and Magog, and so on till the end. So we see that these are not reconcile-able with how the Professor is presenting it. Especially as the professor mentions, that each one of these eschatologies is really only understood or put into action by the most “extreme” (or maybe we can say active) constituents of each religious group. So I don’t believe they will abandon literalism to their religious prophecies in favor of a metaphorical stretch will really opposes what each side is trying to achieve by itself. 

The professor presents his theory very interestingly, but I think fails to acknowledge what will happen in the game if one eschatology seems to have gotten it completely right (like exactly everything happens without metaphorizing the prophecies), which would impact how others factions would align or oppose with the group with the prevailing (accurate) eschatology. Another important consideration is what happens to the large groups of followers of each eschatology if they see their respective prophecies not materialize, do they abandon ship and go towards atheism/individualism, or do they join another faction? I think the scope of the professor’s theory is too short. He played with the idea of Putin passing away, and earlier about Erdogan. If we assume a slightly longer timeframe, just another 50 years, most if not all of these players are gone. And now if we try to set up the game in the future time, it would be much different inputs, considerations, and results. Especially since the prediction is that the stability of Turkey especially under Erdogan, and I believe the stability of Russia under Putin as well is highly reliant on these leaders’ strong ability to maneuver the inner political conflicts within them. 

I would also love to see the professor go more in-depth about Russia’s inner conflicts and it’s future prospects. How can he theorize that Russia ends up being the force/leader of peace for the world, when in other videos he predicts how these “big cities/empires” are bound to collapse. How will one empire manage to run it’s empire successfully as a leader of peace when the economic considerations point toward inevitable decline and collapse. Unless, by this theory, the professor is predicting that the “rise phase” of the leading empire in eschatology is that golden millennium. Then that would make sense. 

Another problem with the approach I see is that these are end of times predictions. So if any of them were to be real, then the world would be coming to an end and Judgement Day would be shortly thereafter. I feel like this prt was ignored in this lecture. Profesor took the build up to the main event that he could fit together (kind of like Frankenstein eschatology) and then threw everything else away. If you ignore the final result of each eschatology, essentially assuming it does not actually happen, I would imagine that has huge impacts on the actions of each group and whether or not they would still believe in them or acting in the interest of their particular group. ### **@HashOut**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YQ-xg1nIbMs&lc=UgzKCMILtqsye56ESEV4AaABAg)

Russia will not become a superpower in the coming years, for several key reasons:

🇺🇦 They are losing in Ukraine: Despite early expectations of a quick victory, Russia is bogged down in a costly, drawn-out war with heavy losses in manpower, equipment, and global influence.

📉 Shrinking economy: Sanctions, capital flight, and dependence on energy exports have pushed Russia into economic isolation. It’s now considered a second-tier economy with limited innovation or diversification.

👵 Demographic crisis: Russia's population is aging and shrinking. The war has worsened this by accelerating emigration and causing massive military casualties among young men.

🌍 Loss of global credibility: Putin and the Russian leadership have lost trust and legitimacy on the world stage. Even historically neutral countries now view Russia as a threat.

🛑 Technological stagnation: Sanctions have cut off access to critical tech imports and investment. Russia now lags significantly in key sectors like semiconductors, AI, and green energy.

🪖 Overreliance on military force: Soft power is virtually nonexistent. Russia depends almost entirely on coercion and intimidation, which alienates neighbors and deters alliances.

🇨🇳 Dependent on China: Russia is increasingly reliant on China economically and diplomatically, essentially making it a junior partner rather than a global leader.

Being a superpower requires more than nukes and oil. Russia lacks the economic, demographic, diplomatic, and technological foundation to reassert itself as a dominant global force.

As to reliance of oil import, Japan imports 100% of her needs whereas China imports 75% and Japan has not yet collapsed. As to food import, China again grows sufficient grain for human consumption and imports other produces (such as soybean) for animal feeds. Even if China stopped importing such produces, Chinese won't starve but choices for protein may become more restricted. The situation is again not so much different from Japan. Hence it makes no sense that you believe Japan will be a dominating power in East Asia and yet China will collapse.
